\textstyle{{\operatorname{Mor}}_{w}{\mathscr{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Next we prove a variant of Theorem
9.3
.

Proposition 9.7
.

Let
(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences and strong duality.
Assume that the underlying exact category with weak equivalences and duality
(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can})
has a strong symmetric cone.
Then the commutative square of duality preserving inclusions of exact form categories with weak equivalences

\textstyle{(\mathscr{E}^{w},i,Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathscr{E}^{w},w,Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathscr{E},i,Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathscr{E},w,Q)}

induces a
homotopy cartesian square of associated
Grothendieck-Witt spaces where the weak equivalences of the left two form categories are the isomorphisms.
Moreover, the upper right
corner has contractible Grothendieck-Witt space.

Proof.

Replacing
[
Sch10b
, Proposition 4]
with Proposition
9.6
, the proof is
mutatis mutandis
the same as that of
[
Sch10b
, Proposition 5]
.
Let
\operatorname{Fun}^{1}_{w}(\underline{n},E)\subset\operatorname{Fun}_{w}(\underline{n},E)
be the full form subcategory of those objects

for which
A_{p}\to A_{q}
is an admissible monomorphism and
A_{q^{\prime}}\to A_{p^{\prime}}
is an admissible epimorphism for
0\leq p\leq q\leq n
.
The main point is to show that this form functor inclusion induces an equivalence of Grothendieck-Witt spaces.
The inverse is provided by the difference
GW(F)-GW(G)
for two form functors
F,G:\operatorname{Fun}_{w}(\underline{n},E)\to\operatorname{Fun}^{1}_{w}(\underline{n},E)
defined in
[

, Proof of Proposition 5]
.
We need to say what

and
G
do on quadratic forms.
Note that a quadratic form on

is a pair

where

and

such that

.
As in
[
Sch10b
]
we illustrate the case

.
Recall that

, and

sends that object to

.
On quadratic forms, the form functors

and

are defined by

\begin{array}[]{ccc}Q\left(E_{1^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\to}}E_{0^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\to}}E_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\to}}E_{1}\right)&\longrightarrow&Q\left(E_{1^{\prime}}\oplus PE_{0^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a,p)}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}E_{0^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\to}}E_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}c\\
i\end{smallmatrix}\right)}}{{\rightarrowtail}}E_{1}\oplus CE_{0}\right)\\
(q,\beta)&\mapsto&(q,\beta^{F})\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{ccc}Q\left(E_{1^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\to}}E_{0^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\to}}E_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\to}}E_{1}\right)&\longrightarrow&Q\left(PE_{0^{\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow 0\to 0\rightarrowtail CE_{0}\right)\\
(q,\beta)&\mapsto&(0,\beta^{G})\end{array}

where
\beta^{F}
and

are as in
[
Sch10b
]
, that is,

\beta^{F}_{1^{\prime}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\beta_{1^{\prime}}&0\\
0&\gamma_{E_{0}}\circ P\beta_{0^{\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\hskip 8.61108pt\beta^{F}_{0^{\prime}}=\beta_{0^{\prime}},\hskip 8.61108pt\beta^{F}_{0}=\beta_{0},\hskip 8.61108pt\beta^{F}_{1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\beta_{1}&0\\
0&\tilde{\gamma}_{E_{0^{\prime}}}\circ C\beta_{0}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

and

\beta^{G}_{1^{\prime}}=\gamma_{E_{0}}\circ P\beta_{0^{\prime}},\hskip 8.61108pt\beta^{G}_{1}=\tilde{\gamma}_{E_{0^{\prime}}}\circ C\beta_{0}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
9.3
.

The proof now is the same is that of
[
Sch10b
, Theorem 6]
replacing Lemma 2, Lemma 4 and Proposition 5 of
[
Sch10b
]
with Lemma
7.7
, Lemma
8.2
and Proposition
9.7
.
∎

Theorem 9.8

(Cofinality)
.

Let
(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences which has a strong symmetric cone.
Let
A\subset K_{0}(\mathscr{E},w)
be a subgroup closed under the duality action on

K_{0}(\mathscr{E},w)
, and let
\mathscr{E}_{A}\subset\mathscr{E}
be the full
subcategory of those objects whose class in
K_{0}(\mathscr{E},w)
belongs to
A
.
Then the category
\mathscr{E}_{A}
inherits the structure of an exact form category with weak
equivalences from
(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
, and
the induced map on Grothendieck Witt spaces

GW(\mathscr{E}_{A},w,Q)\longrightarrow GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)

is an isomorphism on

\pi_{i}
,
i\geq 1
, and a monomorphism
on
\pi_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of
[
Sch10b
, Theorem 7]
replacing Lemma 2, Lemma 4, Proposition 5 and the reference to
[
Sch10a
, Corollary 5.2]
in
[
Sch10b
]
with Lemma
7.7
, Lemma
8.2
, Proposition
9.7
and Theorem
4.2
.
∎

To finish the section we record the following corollary of Theorem
9.3
.

Corollary 9.9
.

Assume the hypothesis of Theorem
9.3
.
Then for all
n\geq 1
, the commutative square (
9.2
) of exact form categories with weak equivalences induces a homotopy cartesian square with contractible upper right corner after application of the functor

|w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(n)}(\phantom{E})|
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
9.3
in view of the definition of the Grothendieck-Witt space (in case
n=1
), Corollary
8.7
(for
n\geq 2
) and its
K
-theoretic analogue
[
Wal85
, Theorem 1.6.4]
,
[
Wal85
, Proposition 1.5.3]
and the fact that barycentric subdivision does not change homotopy type
[
Sch10b
, Lemma 1]
.
∎

10.
From exact categories to chain complexes

Let
(\mathscr{E},{\sharp},\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with strong duality.
We will make the category
\operatorname{Ch}_{b}(\mathscr{E})
of bounded chain complexes
in
\mathscr{E}
into an exact form category with weak equivalences
(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
such that
(\mathscr{E},Q)
and
(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},Q)
have homotopy equivalent Grothendieck-Witt spaces (Theorem
10.5
).
The structure of exact category with weak equivalences and duality on
\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
is standard
[
Sch10b
, §6]
, and our task is to equip
\operatorname{Ch}_{b}(\mathscr{E})
with the correct quadratic functor; see Definition
10.2
.

In this paper, we use homological indexing.
So, an object of
\operatorname{Ch}_{b}(\mathscr{E})
is a chain complex in
\mathscr{E}
, that is, a diagram in
\mathscr{E}

(E,d):\hskip 17.22217pt\cdots\to E_{n+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{n+1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{n-1}\to\cdots,

n\in\mathbb{Z}
, such that
E_{n}=0
for
|n|>>0
and
d_{n}d_{n+1}=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
A morphism of chain complexes, also called chain map,
f:(A,d)\to(B,d)
is a family of maps
f_{n}:A_{n}\to B_{n}
in
\mathscr{E}
such that
f_{n-1}d_{n}=d_{n}f_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
I will denote by
\mathscr{E}((A,d),(B,d))
, or simply
\mathscr{E}(A,B)
if the differentials
d
are understood, the abelian group of chain maps
(A,d)\to(B,d)
in
\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
.
A sequence

(A,d)\to(B,d)\to(C,d)
of chain complexes in
\mathscr{E}
is called
exact
if the sequence
A_{n}\rightarrowtail B_{n}\twoheadrightarrow C_{n}
is admissible exact in
\mathscr{E}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Call a chain complex
(E,d)
in
\mathscr{E}

strictly
acyclic
if every
differential
d_{n}
is the composition
E_{n}\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Im}d_{n}\rightarrowtail E_{n-1}
of an admissible epimorphism followed by an admissible monomorphism, and the
sequences
\operatorname{Im}d_{n+1}\rightarrowtail E_{n}\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Im}d_{n}
are
admissible exact in
\mathscr{E}
.
A chain complex is called
acyclic
if it is homotopy equivalent to a
strictly acyclic chain complex.
A chain map
f:(A,d)\to(B,d)
is a
quasi-isomorphism
if its cone
C(f)
is acyclic where

C(f)_{n}=B_{n}\oplus A_{n-1}
with differential
C(f)_{n}\to C(f)_{n-1}
given by

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{n}&f\\
0&-d_{n-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right):B_{n}\oplus A_{n-1}\to B_{n-1}\oplus A_{n-2}.

Write
\operatorname{quis}
for the set of quasi-isomorphisms in
\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
.
Then the pair

(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}(\mathscr{E}),\operatorname{quis})

is an exact category with weak equivalences.

The duality functor
({\sharp},\operatorname{can})
on
\mathscr{E}
extends to a duality functor
({\sharp},\operatorname{can})
on
\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
where

(E,d)^{\sharp}=(E^{\sharp},d^{\sharp})
,
f^{\sharp}:(Y,d)^{\sharp}\to(X,d)^{\sharp}
and
\operatorname{can}_{E}:(E,d)\to(E,d)^{{\sharp}{\sharp}}
are given by

\begin{array}[]{lcllcl}(E^{\sharp})_{i}&=&(E_{-i})^{\sharp},&(f^{\sharp})_{i}&=&(f_{-i})^{\sharp},\\
(d^{\sharp})_{i}&=&(-1)^{i+1}(d_{-i+1})^{\sharp},&(\operatorname{can}_{E})_{i}&=&(-1)^{i}\operatorname{can}_{E_{i}}.\end{array}

Remark 10.1
.

We explain the choice of signs in the formula for the duality above.
If
(\mathscr{E},\otimes,[\phantom{A},\phantom{B}])
is a closed symmetric monoidal category with evaluation
e:[A,B]\otimes A\to B
, coevaluation
\triangledown:A\to[B,A\otimes B]
and symmetry
c:A\otimes B\to B\otimes A
.
Then the category of bounded chain complexes
\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
in
\mathscr{E}
has a standard structure of a closed symmetric monoidal category where evaluation and coevaluation are induced by
e
and
\triangledown
without introducing signs, and the symmetry follows the Koszul sign rule.
A standard example is
\mathscr{E}=R\operatorname{proj}
, the category of finitely generated projective modules over a commutative ring
R
; see for instance
[
Sch17
, §1.1]
.

An object
A
of a closed symmetric monoidal category defines a duality
X\mapsto[X,A]
with double dual identification

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangledown}}{{\longrightarrow}}[[X,A],X\otimes[X,A]]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[1,c]}}{{\longrightarrow}}[[X,A],[X,A]\otimes X]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle[1,e]}}{{\longrightarrow}}[[X,A],A].

With our sign choices, if the duality
X\mapsto[X,A]_{\mathscr{E}}
on
\mathscr{E}
comes from a closed symmetric monoidal structure as explained above, then its extension to
\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
is the duality
X\to[X,A]_{\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}}
coming from the closed symmetric monoidal structure on
\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
where
A
is considered as a chain complex concentrated in degree
0
.

Now we come to the crucial definition of quadratic forms on chain complexes.

Definition 10.2
.

Let
(\mathscr{E},{\sharp},\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with strong duality.
A
quadratic form on a chain complex

(E,d)\in\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
is a pair
(\xi,\varphi)
, where

\varphi:(E,d)\to(E,d)^{\sharp}
is a map of complexes and

\xi\in Q(E_{0})
is a quadratic form on
E_{0}\in\mathscr{E}
satisfying

\varphi^{\sharp}\operatorname{can}_{E}=\varphi,\hskip 17.22217ptd_{1}^{\bullet}(\xi)=0,\hskip 17.22217pt\rho(\xi)=\varphi_{0}.

As usual, we denote by
Q(E)
the abelian group of quadratic forms on
E=(E,d)
.
Transfer and restriction

\mathscr{E}(E,E^{\sharp})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q(E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{E}(E,E^{\sharp})

are defined by

\tau(f)=(\tau(f_{0}),f+f^{\sharp}\operatorname{can}_{E})
and
\rho(\xi,\varphi)=\varphi
.
Note that
\tau(f)
is indeed a quadratic form on the chain complex
E
since

d_{1}^{\bullet}(\tau(f_{0}))=\tau((d_{1})^{\sharp}f_{0}d_{1})=\tau(-f_{-1}d_{0}d_{1})=0

and

\rho(\tau(f_{0}))=f_{0}+(f_{0})^{\sharp}\operatorname{can}_{E_{0}}
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
(\mathscr{E},{\sharp},\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with strong duality.
Then the tuple

(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},{\sharp},\operatorname{can},Q)

is an exact form category with weak equivalences and strong duality which has a strong symmetric cone.

Proof.

It is standard that
(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},{\sharp},\operatorname{can})
is an exact category with weak equivalences and strong duality which has a strong symmetric cone
[
Sch10b
, §6]
.
Thus, it remains to show that the functor
(Q,\tau,\rho)
is quadratic left exact.
For the convenience of the reader we will also spell out the symmetric cone below.

It is clear that
\tau
and
\rho
are
C_{2}
-equivariant, functorial in
E\in\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
and that
\rho\tau=1+\sigma
.
For chain maps
f,g:(A,d)\to(B,d)
we have

\begin{array}[]{rl}&Q(f+g)^{\bullet}(\xi,\varphi)-Q(f)^{\bullet}(\xi,\varphi)-Q(g)^{\bullet}(\xi,\varphi)\\
=&(\tau(f_{0}^{\sharp}\circ\rho(\xi)\circ g_{0}),f^{\sharp}\varphi g+g^{\sharp}\varphi f)\\
=&\tau(f^{\sharp}\circ\rho(\xi,\varphi)\circ g).\end{array}

Thus,
(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
is an additive form category.

We need to check that
Q:(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E})^{op}\to\operatorname{Ab}
is quadratic left exact (
[
Sch21
, Definition 2.22]
).
Let
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\rightarrowtail}}Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}Z
be an admissible exact sequence of bounded chain complexes in
\mathscr{E}
.
Then
p^{\bullet}:Q(Z)\to Q(Y):(\xi,\varphi)\mapsto(p_{0}^{\bullet}\xi,p^{\sharp}\varphi p)
is injective because
p_{0}^{\bullet}
and
\varphi\mapsto p^{\sharp}\varphi p
are.
Let
(\xi,\varphi)\in Q(Y)
such that
i^{\sharp}\circ\varphi=0
and
i_{0}^{\bullet}(\xi)=0
.
Then there are unique
\zeta\in Q(Z_{0})
and
\psi:Z\to Z^{\sharp}
such that
\xi=p_{0}^{\bullet}(\zeta)
and
\varphi=p^{\sharp}\psi p
since
Q:\mathscr{E}^{op}\to\operatorname{Ab}
and
(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E})^{op}\to\operatorname{Ab}:E\mapsto\mathscr{E}(E,E^{\sharp})^{C_{2}}
are quadratic left exact functors.
Note that
\psi^{\sharp}\operatorname{can}_{Z}=\psi
,
d_{1}^{\bullet}(\zeta)=0
and
\rho(\zeta)=\psi_{0}
since

\psi\mapsto p^{\sharp}\psi p
is
C_{2}
-equivariant and injective,
p_{1}^{\bullet}d_{1}^{\bullet}(\zeta)=d_{1}^{\bullet}p_{0}^{\bullet}(\zeta)=d_{1}^{\bullet}(\xi)=0
and
p_{1}^{\bullet}
is injective, and
p_{0}^{\sharp}\rho(\zeta)p_{0}=\rho(p_{0}^{\bullet}\zeta)=\rho(\xi)=\varphi_{0}=p_{0}^{\sharp}\psi_{0}p_{0}
.
Thus,
(\zeta,\psi)\in Q(Z)
satisfies
p^{\bullet}(\zeta,\psi)=(\xi,\varphi)
and
Q:(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E})^{op}\to\operatorname{Ab}
is quadratic left exact.

A conceptual proof of the existence of a strong symmetric cone was given in
[
Sch10b
, §7.5]
.
Here, we spell out the formulas in detail (switching the order of tensor product compared to
loc. cit.
).
For a chain complex
E=(E,d)
, cone
CE
and path object
PE
are defined as usual by
(CE)_{n}=E_{n}\oplus E_{n-1}
and
(PE)_{n}=E_{n}\oplus E_{n+1}
with differentials

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{n}&1\\
0&-d_{n-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right):(CE)_{n}\to(CE)_{n-1}\hskip 12.91663pt\text{and}\hskip 12.91663pt\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{n}&0\\
1&-d_{n+1}\end{smallmatrix}\right):(PE)_{n}\to(PE)_{n-1}.

They are equipped with the natural maps
i:E\rightarrowtail CE
and
p:PE\twoheadrightarrow E
which in degree
n
are

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right):E_{n}\to E_{n}\oplus E_{n-1}\hskip 17.22217pt\text{and}\hskip 17.22217pt(1\ \ 0):E_{n}\oplus E_{n+1}\to E_{n}.

Finally, the natural transformation
\gamma_{E}:P\sharp E\to\sharp CE
in degree
n
is the isomorphism

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&(-1)^{n}\end{smallmatrix}\right):(E_{-n})^{\sharp}\oplus(E_{-n-1})^{\sharp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}(E_{-n})^{\sharp}\oplus(E_{-n-1})^{\sharp}.

∎

For an exact form category with strong duality
(\mathscr{E},{\sharp},\operatorname{can},Q)
,
the inclusion
G:\mathscr{E}\to\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
as complexes concentrated in degree
0
, defines a fully faithful
exact form functor

(10.1)

(G,1,1):(\mathscr{E},i,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)\to(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q).

Lemma 10.4
.

Assume that
\mathscr{E}
is semi-idempotent complete.
Then the form functor (
10.1
) induces a surjective map of Grothendieck-Witt groups

GW_{0}(\mathscr{E},i,Q)\twoheadrightarrow GW_{0}(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},Q).

Proof.

Recall from Theorem
8.4
that
GW_{0}(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},Q)
is generated by symbols
[E,\xi,\varphi]
where

E
is a bounded chain complex in
\mathscr{E}
and
(\xi,\varphi)\in Q(E)
is a quadratic form on
E
with
\varphi
a quasi-isomorphism.

Let
n\geq 2
be an integer and let
[E,\xi,\varphi]
be one of those generators.
Assume that
E_{i}=0
for
i>n
and
i<-n
.
Since the cone of
\varphi
is acyclic (hence strictly acyclic, by
semi-idempotent completeness of
\mathscr{E}
), the map

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{n}\\
\phi_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\right):E_{n}\to E_{n-1}\oplus E_{-n}^{\sharp}

is an admissible monomorphism.
Define a complex
\tilde{E}
supported in
[-n,n]
by

E_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{n}\\
\varphi_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}}{{\rightarrowtail}}E_{n-1}\oplus E_{-n}^{\sharp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{n-2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}E_{-n+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{-n+1}\oplus E_{-n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{-n+1}&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}E_{-n}.

The complex
\tilde{E}
is equipped with a non-singular quadratic form

(\xi,\tilde{\varphi})
where
\tilde{\varphi}=\varphi_{i}
for all
i
except in degrees
i=-n+1,n-1
where
\tilde{\varphi}_{n-1}=\varphi_{n-1}\oplus 1
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{-n+1}=\varphi_{-n+1}\oplus\operatorname{can}_{E_{-n}}
.
Note that
(\tilde{E},\xi,\tilde{\varphi})
is indeed non-singular since the cone of
\tilde{\varphi}
is homotopy equivalent to the cone of
\varphi
which is acyclic.
The bicartesian square

(E_{n}\to E_{n-1}\oplus E_{-n}^{\sharp}\to\cdots E_{-n+1}\to E_{-n})
\textstyle{\tilde{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
(E_{n}\to E_{n-1}\to\cdots E_{-n+1}\oplus E_{-n}\to E_{-n})

carries an obvious symmetric form induced by
\varphi
.
On the upper left corner, this form is
\varphi
in each degree, except in degree
n-1
where it is
\varphi_{n-1}\oplus 1
.
The cone of that chain map is homotopy equivalent to the cone of
\varphi
and thus acyclic.
In particular the symmetric form on the square is non-singular.
In the square, the quadratic forms
(\xi,\tilde{\varphi})
and
(\xi,\varphi)
on
\tilde{E}
and
E
restrict to the same form on the upper left corner.
Therefore,

[E,\xi,\varphi]=[\tilde{E},\xi,\tilde{\varphi}]-[H(E_{-n})[-n]]\in GW_{0}(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},Q).

Similarly, define the complex
E^{\prime}
supported in
[-n+1,n-1]
by

0\to\operatorname{coker}{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{n}\\
\varphi_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{n-2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}E_{-n+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ker{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{-n+1}&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\to 0.

We have a diagram of complexes

E^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}}\left(\text{\footnotesize$E_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{n}\\
\varphi_{n}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{n-1}\oplus E_{-n}^{\sharp}\to\cdots\to E_{-n+2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ker{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{-n+1}&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\to 0$ }\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{{}{\hbox{\kern 10.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}}}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}}\tilde{E}

where the left arrow is the canonical quotient map and the right arrow is the canonical inclusion.
Both arrows are quasi-isomorphisms since kernel and cokernel are acyclic.
Moreover, the quadratic form

(\xi,\tilde{\varphi})
on
\tilde{E}
restricted to the middle complex uniquely descends to a quadratic form
(\xi^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
on
E^{\prime}
, by quadratic left exactness of
Q
.
It follows that

[E,\xi,\varphi]=[\tilde{E},\xi,\tilde{\varphi}]-[H(E_{-n})]=[E^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}]-[H(E_{-n})]\in GW_{0}(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},Q)

The same argument works for
n=1
provided we define
\tilde{E}
concentrated in
[-1,1]
and
E^{\prime}
concentrated in degree
0
as

E_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}d_{1}\\
0\\
\varphi_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}}{{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{{}{\hbox{\kern 10.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}}}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}}E_{0}\oplus E_{-1}\oplus E_{-1}^{\sharp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d_{0}\ 1\ 0)}}{{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}}E_{-1}\hskip 12.91663pt\text{and}\hskip 12.91663ptH_{0}(\tilde{E}).

The chain complex
\tilde{E}
is equipped with the quadratic form
(\xi\perp h_{E_{-1}},\tilde{\varphi})
where
h_{E_{-1}}
is the hyperbolic quadratic form on
E_{-1}
,
\tilde{\varphi}_{1}=\varphi_{1}
,

\tilde{\varphi}_{0}=\varphi_{0}\perp\rho(h_{E_{-1}})
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{-1}=\varphi_{-1}
.
The quadratic form on
E^{\prime}
is the unique form which agrees with the form on
\tilde{E}
when restricted to the middle complex in the diagram of quasi-isomorphisms

E^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}}\left(E_{1}\rightarrowtail\ker(d_{0}\ 1\ 0)\to 0\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{{}{\hbox{\kern 10.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}}}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}}\tilde{E}.

To finish the proof, by induction, any generator
[{E},\xi,{\varphi}]
is a sum of hyperbolic spaces and a space
[E^{\prime},\xi^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}]
with
E^{\prime}
concentrated in degree
0
.
Then the quasi-isomorphism
\varphi^{\prime}
is an isomorphism, and
[E,\xi,\varphi]
is in the image of
GW_{0}(\mathscr{E},i,Q)\to GW_{0}(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},Q)
.
∎

The following is the main result of this paper.
In the case of symmetric forms it was proved in

[
Sch10b
, Proposition 6]
.

Theorem 10.5
.

For an exact form category with strong duality
(\mathscr{E},{\sharp},\operatorname{can},Q)
,
the form functor (
10.1
)
induces a homotopy equivalence of Grothendieck-Witt spaces

GW(\mathscr{E},i,Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}GW(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},Q).

Proof.

By Lemma
10.6
below, we can assume that
\mathscr{E}
is semi-idempotent complete and that therefore acyclic complexes in
\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
are strictly acyclic.
Let
\operatorname{Ac}_{b}\mathscr{E}\subset\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
be the full exact form subcategory of acyclic complexes, and
for
n\geq 0
, let
\operatorname{Ac}_{[-n,n]}\mathscr{E}\subset\operatorname{Ac}_{b}\mathscr{E}
and
\operatorname{Ch}_{[-n,n]}\mathscr{E}\subset\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E}
be the full exact form subcategories of complexes
E
satisfying
E_{i}=0
for
i>n
and
i<-n
.
For
n\geq 1
, consider the commutative diagram of exact form categories with weak equivalences the isomorphisms

(10.2)

\textstyle{\operatorname{Ac}_{[-n+1,n-1]}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ac}_{[-n,n]}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ch}_{[-n+1,n-1]}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ch}_{[-n,n]}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E}}

where the form functors in the left square are the obvious inclusions and the right horizontal form functors send a complex
E
to
(E_{n},E_{-n}^{\sharp})
with duality compatibility
(1,\operatorname{can}_{E_{n}}):(E_{-n}^{\sharp},E_{n}^{\sharp\sharp})\to(E_{-n}^{\sharp},E_{n})
.
The rows of diagram (
10.2
) induce (split) homotopy fibrations of
GW
-spaces in view of the Additivity Theorem
8.1

applied to the bicartesian squares of form functors
sending
A\in\operatorname{Ac}_{[-n,n]}\mathscr{E}
and
B\in\operatorname{Ch}_{[-n,n]}\mathscr{E}
to

\textstyle{(A_{n}\to A_{n-1}\to\cdots\to\ker d_{-n+1}\to 0)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(A_{n}\to A_{n-1}\to\cdots\to A_{-n+1}\to A_{-n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(0\to\operatorname{Im}d_{n-1}\to\cdots\to\ker d_{-n+1}\to 0)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(0\to\operatorname{Im}d_{n-1}\to\cdots A_{n-1}\to A_{n})}

and

\textstyle{(0\to B_{n-1}\to\cdots\to B_{-n+1}\to B_{-n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(B_{n}\to B_{n-1}\to\cdots\to B_{-n+1}\to B_{-n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(0\to B_{n-1}\to\cdots\to B_{-n+1}\to 0)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(B_{n}\to B_{n-1}\to\cdots B_{n-1}\to 0)}

equipped with the unique forms which are the identity on the upper right corners.
Note that the condition
d_{1}^{\bullet}(\xi)=0
is used to make sense of the diagram of form functors for
A\in\operatorname{Ac}_{[-1,1]}=S_{2}\mathscr{E}
.
It follows that the left square in (
10.2
) is homotopy cartesian.
Then all squares in

\textstyle{\operatorname{Ac}_{[0,0]}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ac}_{[-n+1,n-1]}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ac}_{[-n,n]}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{colim}_{n}\operatorname{Ac}_{[-n,n]}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ch}_{[0,0]}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ch}_{[-n+1,n-1]}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ch}_{[-n,n]}\mathscr{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{colim}_{n}\operatorname{Ch}_{[-n,n]}\mathscr{E}}

including the outer square are homotopy cartesian.
In other words, the left square in

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\operatorname{Ac}^{b}\mathscr{E},i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\operatorname{Ac}^{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathscr{E},i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\operatorname{Ch}^{b}\mathscr{E},i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\operatorname{Ch}^{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis}).}

induces a homotopy cartesian square of
GW
-spaces.
By Proposition
9.7
, the right square also induces a homotopy cartesian square of
GW
-spaces.
Hence, the total square does, too.
Since the GW-space of the upper right corner is contranctible, the homotopy fibre of the lower horizontal map is trivial, that is, the map

GW_{i}(\mathscr{E},i,Q)\to GW_{i}(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},Q)
is an isomorphism for
i>0
and a monomorphism for
i=0
.
By Lemma
10.4
, the map is also surjectve for
i=0
.
This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences and strong
duality, then the inclusion
\mathscr{E}\subset\tilde{\mathscr{E}}^{0}
into its semi-idempotent completion induces a
homotopy equivalence of Grothendieck-Witt spaces

GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)\to GW(\tilde{\mathscr{E}}^{0},w,Q).

Proof.

Using Theorem
4.2
and Example
4.3
, the proof now is the same as
[
Sch10b
, Lemma 12]
.
∎

11.
The Grothendieck-Witt spectrum and the Bott sequence

In this section we will generalise various results from
[
Sch17
]
.
We will construct semi-nonconnective delooping of the
GW
-space giving rise to a spectrum
GW
generalising
[
Sch17
, §5]
.
This allows us to generalize the Bott sequence
[
Sch17
, Theorem 6.1]
.

Let
(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences.
Recall that the category
\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}=\operatorname{Fun}([1],\mathscr{E})
of morphisms in
\mathscr{E}
is canonically equipped with the structure of an exact form category; see the paragraph before Proposition
9.6
.
The dual of an object
f:X\to Y
of
\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}
is
f^{\sharp}:Y^{\sharp}\to X^{\sharp}
, the double dual identification of
f
is
(\operatorname{can}_{X},\operatorname{can}_{Y})
,
the set of quadratic forms
Q(f:X\to Y)
on
f
is the set of pairs
(\xi,a)
where
\xi\in Q(X)
is a quadratic form on
X
and
a:X\to Y^{\sharp}
is an arrow in
\mathscr{E}
such that
f^{\sharp}a=\rho(\xi)
.
A sequence in
\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}
is admissibly exact if the sequences of source objects and target objects are admissibly exact in
\mathscr{E}
.
A morphism in
\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}
is a weak equivalence it the maps of source and target objects are weak equivalences in
\mathscr{E}
.
If
\mathscr{E}
has a strong symmetric cone, then so has
\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}
, by functoriality of the symmetric cone of
\mathscr{E}
.

Consider the exact form functor

(11.1)

F:\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E}:(f:X\to Y)\mapsto(X,Y^{\sharp})

with duality compatibility map
(1,\operatorname{can}):F(f^{\sharp})\to F(f)^{\sharp}
and map on quadratic forms

Q_{\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}}(f)\to Q_{\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E}}(X,Y^{\sharp})=\mathscr{E}(X,Y^{\sharp}):(\xi,a)\mapsto a.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences.
Then for any integer
n\geq 1
, the functor (
11.1
) induces a homotopy equivalence of pointed topological spaces

|w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}^{(n)}(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},Q)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}|w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}^{(n)}(\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E})|.

Proof.

Since the lemma holds for
K
-theory (same proof as below), we are reduced to showing that (
11.1
) induces a homotopy equivalence on Grothendieck-Witt spaces, in view of Corollary
8.7
.
The inverse of (
11.1
) on Grothendieck-Witt spaces is given by the form functor

(11.2)

(G,\psi):\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E}\to\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}:(X,Y)\mapsto(0:X\to Y^{\sharp})

with duality compatibility map
(\operatorname{can}_{Y},1_{X^{\sharp}}):G(Y,X)\to G(X,Y)^{\sharp}

and map on quadratic forms

Q_{\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E}}(X,Y)=\mathscr{E}(X,Y)\longrightarrow Q_{\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}}(X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0}}{{\to}}Y^{\sharp}):a\mapsto(0,a)

This is because
(1,\operatorname{can}):id_{\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E}}\to F\circ G
is a natural weak equivalence of form functors and thus induces a homotopy of associated maps on Grothendieck-Witt spaces by Lemma
7.7
..
Moreover,
G\circ F
is naturally weakly equivalent to the duality preserving form functor
(f:X\to Y)\mapsto(0:X\to Y)
which on quadratic forms is
Q(f:X\to Y)\to Q(0:X\to Y):(\xi,a)\mapsto(0,a)
. Up to homotopy, this form functor induces the same map on Grothendieck-Witt spaces as the identity functor, by the Additivity Theorem,
in view of the natural short exact sequence in
\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}

(0\to Y)\to(f:X\to Y)\to(X\to 0).

∎

Consider the sequence of functors

(11.3)

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},Q)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(E,\xi)\mapsto E}}{{\longrightarrow}}&w\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}&&\\
&\longrightarrow&w\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}\mathscr{E}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E\mapsto E\iota}}{{\longrightarrow}}&wS^{(n)}_{\bullet}\mathscr{E}\end{array}

in which the non-labelled map is the functor
\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}=\operatorname{Fun}([1],\mathscr{E})\to\operatorname{Fun}([0],\mathscr{E})=\mathscr{E}
induced by the inclusion
[0]\to[1]:0\mapsto 0
and the last functor is induced by the inclusion
\iota:[p]\subset[p]^{op}[p]
.

Corollary 11.2
.

For every integer
n\geq 1
and every exact form category with weak equivalences
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
, the composition of the maps in (
11.3
) induces a homotopy equivalence of pointed spaces

|w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},Q)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}|wS^{(n)}_{\bullet}\mathscr{E}|.

Proof.

The composition in (
11.3
) factors as

w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle F}}{{\longrightarrow}}w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{H}w\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}\mathscr{E})_{h}\longrightarrow w\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}\mathscr{E}\longrightarrow wS^{(n)}_{\bullet}\mathscr{E}

where
\mathcal{C}_{h}
denotes the category of symmetric forms in a category with duality
\mathcal{C}
as defined in
[
Sch10b
, §2.1]
.
The first map induces a homotopy equivalence by Lemma
11.1
, the second map, by
[
Sch10b
, §2 Lemma 3]
, and the third map, by
[
Sch10b
, §2 Lemma 1]
.
∎

Let
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences which has a strong symmetric cone.
We denote by

\mathscr{E}^{[1]}=(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w_{cone},\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)

the exact form category with weak equivalences which has underlying exact form category
(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
and where the weak equivalences are the maps
f\to g
in
\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}
which induce a weak equivalence
C(f)\to C(g)
in
\mathscr{E}
on cones.
Denote by
\operatorname{cone}:\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{E}^{[1]}
the change of weak-equivalence form functor

(11.4)

\operatorname{cone}:(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)\to(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w_{cone},\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)

which is the identity on underlying categories and quadratic functors.
The functor
I:\mathscr{E}\to\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}
sending an object
E
to the identity arrow
1_{E}
on
E
makes

(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
into a fully faithful exact form subcategory of
(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
with image in the form subcategory
(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)^{\operatorname{cone}}
of those objects
f
of
\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}
for which
0\to C(f)
is a weak equivalence in
\mathscr{E}
.

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences which has a strong symmetric cone.
Then the fully faithful inclusion of form categories

I:(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)\longrightarrow(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)^{\operatorname{cone}}

induces homotopy equivalences of
GW
-spaces and for
n\geq 1
after applying the functor
|w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}(\phantom{E})|
.

Proof.

On Grothendieck-Witt spaces, the form functor

F:(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E})^{\operatorname{cone}}\to\mathscr{E}:(f:X\to Y)\mapsto X

with duality compatibility

f^{\sharp}:F(\sharp f)=Y^{\sharp}\to\sharp F(f)=X^{\sharp}
which on abelian groups of quadratic forms is the map

Q_{\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}}(f)\to Q_{\mathscr{E}}(F(f)):(\xi,a)\mapsto\xi

defines an inverse up to homotopy of
I
in view of the
identity
F\circ I=id
and the
natural weak equivalences of form functors
I\circ F\to id

given by
(1_{X},f)
for
(f:Y\to X)\in\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}
; see Lemma
9.2
.
Similarly,
I
induces an equivalence of
K
-theory spaces, and hence of
|w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}^{(n)}(\phantom{\mathcal{A}})|
spaces in view of Corollary
8.7
.
∎

By functoriality, if
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
has a strong symmetric cone then
\mathscr{E}^{[1]}
also has a strong symmetric cone.
Setting
\mathscr{E}^{[0]}=\mathscr{E}
, we can define recursively for
r\geq 0
the exact form categories with weak equivalences

\mathscr{E}^{[r+1]}=(\mathscr{E}^{[r]})^{[1]}.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences and strong symmetric cone.
Then we have the commutative diagram

(11.5)

\textstyle{w\operatorname{Quad}\left(\mathcal{A},Q\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}\left(\mathcal{A},Q\right)\hskip 4.30554pt\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip-9.04166ptI}
\textstyle{w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}\left(\operatorname{Mor}\mathcal{A},Q\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Cone}}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 18.08331pt\sim}

(
11.3
)
\textstyle{wS_{\bullet}\mathcal{A}}
\textstyle{w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}\left((\mathcal{A}^{[1]})^{w},Q\right)\hskip 4.30554pt\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}\left(\mathcal{A}^{[1]},Q\right).}

Let
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences and strong symmetric cone.
For
n\geq 0
, replacing
\mathcal{A}
above with

\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}\mathscr{E}^{[n]}=\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}\left(\mathscr{E}^{[n]},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q\right)

we obtain the commutative diagram of (multi-) simplicial categories

(11.6)

\textstyle{w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}\left(\mathscr{E}^{[n]},Q\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(1+n)}_{\bullet}\left(\mathscr{E}^{[n]},Q\right)\hskip 3.87498pt\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip-8.13744ptI}
\textstyle{w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(1+n)}_{\bullet}\left(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}^{[n]},Q\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Cone}}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.27487pt\sim}

(
11.3
)
\textstyle{wS^{(1+n)}_{\bullet}\mathscr{E}^{[n]}}
\textstyle{w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(1+n)}_{\bullet}\left((\mathscr{E}^{[1+n]})^{w},Q\right)\hskip 3.87498pt\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(1+n)}_{\bullet}\left(\mathscr{E}^{[1+n]},Q\right).}

Proposition 11.4
.

Let
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences and strong symmetric cone.
Then, after topological realization, in diagram (
11.6
),
the lower square is homotopy cartesian
with contractible lower left corner for
n\geq 0
, and
the upper square
is homotopy cartesian
for
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

For
n\geq 1
, the upper square is homotopy cartesian, in view of Corollary
8.7
.
The lower square is homotopy cartesian, by Corollary
9.9
applied to the change of weak equivalence form functor (
11.4
) where we use Lemma
11.3
to identify the upper left corner of the square.
∎

Diagram (
11.6
) defines canonical maps for
n\geq 0

(11.7)

\left|w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}\left(\mathscr{E}^{[n]},Q\right)\right|\longrightarrow\Omega\left|w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(1+n)}_{\bullet}\left(\mathscr{E}^{[1+n]},Q\right)\right|.

Actually, the map is a functorial zigzag of maps where the arrows in the wrong direction are homotopy equivalences.

Definition 11.5

(Grothendieck-Witt spectrum)
.

Let
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences and strong symmetric cone.
The
Grothendieck-Witt spetrum of
\mathscr{E}
is the sequence of spaces

GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)=\left\{GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)_{0},\ GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)_{1},\ GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)_{2},\ \dots\right\}

where

\ GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)_{n}=|w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}^{(n)}_{\bullet}\left(\mathscr{E}^{[n]},Q\right)|

together with the bonding maps defined in (
11.7
).
For
n\geq 0
we set

GW^{[n]}(\mathscr{E},w,Q)=GW(\mathscr{E}^{[n]},w,Q).

As usual, we define the
n
-th shifted Grothendieck-Witt groups as the homotopy groups of the Grothendiek-Witt spectrum

GW^{[n]}_{i}(\mathscr{E},w,Q)=\pi_{i}GW^{[n]}(\mathscr{E},w,Q)

for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By Theorem
11.7
below, these groups coincide with the homotopy groups of the Grothendieck-Witt space for
i\geq 0
.

Definition 11.6
.

Let
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with strong duality (equipped with isomorphisms as weak equivalences), we set

GW^{[n]}(\mathscr{E},Q)=GW^{[n]}(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)

where the form category with weak equivalences
(\operatorname{Ch}_{b}\mathscr{E},\operatorname{quis},\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
was defined in §
10
.
By Theorem
11.7
below and Theorem
10.5
, its infinite loop space is the Grothendieck-Witt space of
[
Sch21
, Definition 6.3]
which is the group completion of
|w\operatorname{Quad}\left(\mathscr{E},Q\right)|
in case all admissible exact sequences in
\mathscr{E}
split

[
Sch21
, Theorem 6.5]
.

Theorem 11.7
.

Let
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences and strong symmetric cone.
Then the maps in (
11.7
) are homotopy equivalences for
n\geq 1
.
For
n=0
, the map in (
11.7
) factors as

|w\operatorname{Quad}\left(\mathscr{E},Q\right)|\longrightarrow GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega|w\operatorname{Quad}\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}\left(\mathscr{E}^{[1]},Q\right)|

where the first map is the universal map (
7.5
) and the second is a homotopy equivalence.
In particular, the spectrum
GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)
of Definition
11.5
is a positive
\Omega
-spectrum with associated infinite loop space the Grothendieck-Witt space
GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)
of Definition
7.6
.

Proof.

For
n\geq 1
this is Proposition
11.4
, and for
n=0
, this is Proposition
11.4
together with the definition of the Grothendieck-Witt space.
∎

Lemma 11.8
.

Let
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences equipped with a strong symmetric cone
(C,P)
.
Then the exact functor functor

(11.8)

(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w_{cone})\to(\mathscr{E},w):f\mapsto C(f)

induces an equivalence of
K
-theory spaces with inverse given by

(11.9)

(\mathscr{E},w)\to(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w_{cone}):X\mapsto(0\to X).

Moreover, the map (
11.9
) induces the negative of the map

(11.10)

(\mathscr{E},w)\to(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w_{cone}):X\mapsto(X\to 0)

in
K
-theory.

Proof.

The composition
\mathscr{E}\to\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{E}
is the identity, and the composition
\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}\to\mathscr{E}\to\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E}
is naturally weakly equivalent to the identity by the following zigzag of
w_{cone}
-weak equivalences

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(f)}
\textstyle{C(f).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}

The last claim follows from Additivity in
K
-theory applied to the admissible exact sequence of functors
(\mathscr{E},w)\to(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w_{cone})

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

since the middle functor lands in the subcategory of
w_{cone}
-acyclic objects.
∎

It follows that
\mathscr{E}^{[1]}
and
\mathscr{E}
have the same
K
-theory space, and by iteration, so do
\mathscr{E}^{[n]}
and
\mathscr{E}
.
In particular, for every exact form category with weak equivalences and strong symmetric cone we have the forgetful map

F:GW^{[n]}(\mathscr{E},w,Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Dfn \ref{dfn:HypCatFunEtc}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}K(\mathscr{E}^{[n]},w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(\ref{eqn:EandE[1]inKthCone})}}}{{\longrightarrow}}K(\mathscr{E},w)

and the hyperbolic map

H:K(\mathscr{E},w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(\ref{eqn:EandE[1]inKth})}}}{{\longrightarrow}}K(\mathscr{E}^{[n+1]},w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Dfn \ref{dfn:HypCatFunEtc}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}GW^{[n+1]}(\mathscr{E},w,Q).

Consider the commutative square of exact form categories with weak equivalences.

(11.11)

\textstyle{(\mathscr{E},w,Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{I}
\textstyle{(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w,Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w_{cone},Q)^{w_{cone}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w_{cone},Q)}

The following generalizes
[
Sch17
, Theorem 6.1]
.

Theorem 11.9

(Algebraic Bott sequence)
.

Let
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},w,\sharp,\operatorname{can},Q)
be an exact form category with weak equivalences equipped with a strong symmetric cone.
Then diagram (
11.11
) induces a homotopy fibration of
GW
-spectra for all
n\geq 0

GW^{[n]}(\mathscr{E},w,Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle F}}{{\longrightarrow}}K(\mathscr{E},w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle H}}{{\longrightarrow}}GW^{[n+1]}(\mathscr{E},w,Q).

Proof.

By Corollary
9.9
for
\mathscr{E}^{[n]}
in place of
\mathscr{E}
and
\mathcal{R}^{(n)}
,
diagram (
11.11
) induces a homotopy cartesian square of
GW
-spectra with contractible lower left corner.
In particular, we have a homotopy fibration of spectra

(11.12)

GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)\to GW(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w,Q)\to GW^{[1]}(\mathscr{E},w,Q).

By Lemma
11.1
, the form functor
(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w,Q)\to\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E}
of (
11.1
) induces an equivalence of
GW
-spectra with inverse the form functor (
11.2
).
One observes that the composition

(\mathscr{E},w,Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle I}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w,Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{ (\ref{eqn:FunisH})}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E},w)

is the forgetful functor of Definition
7.8
, and the composition

(\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E},w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{ (\ref{eqn:FunisHinverse})}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w,Q)\longrightarrow(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w_{cone},Q)

is the composition

(\mathscr{H}\mathscr{E},w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(}\ref{eqn:EandE[1]inKthNegative})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{H}(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w_{cone})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle H}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\operatorname{Mor}\mathscr{E},w_{cone},Q).

Thus, the homotopy fibration of spectra (
11.12
) yields the homotopy fibration

GW(\mathscr{E},w,Q)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle F}}{{\longrightarrow}}K(\mathscr{E},w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle-H}}{{\longrightarrow}}GW^{[1]}(\mathscr{E},w,Q).

Replacing
H
with
-H
yields the theorem for
n=0
.
Replacing
\mathscr{E}
with
\mathscr{E}^{[n]}
yields the general case.
∎
